## Turn 0 · _baseline_

### 🟦 User / proxy asks

Why can’t countries with high-valued yet cheap currencies (Eg: 1400 Iraq dinar~1 US dollar) simply remove those zeros?

### 🧠 Reasoning

<details><summary>show reasoning</summary>


We need to answer: "Why can’t countries with high-valued yet cheap currencies (Eg: 1400 Iraq dinar~1 US dollar) simply remove those zeros?"

The user is asking about redenomination, where a country with a devalued currency (like Iraqi dinar, 1 USD = 1400 IQD) might consider lopping off zeros to make the currency seem stronger. The question is why they can't "simply remove those zeros." That implies a redenomination where 1000 old dinars become 1 new dinar. The answer should explain that removing zeros is purely cosmetic; it doesn't change the underlying value of the currency, doesn't fix economic problems, and could cause confusion, costs, and may not address inflation if it's ongoing. Also, there's the issue of psychological effects: it doesn't increase purchasing power. The country still has the same economic fundamentals. So the answer should explain that redenomination is a complex process that doesn't magically make a currency stronger; it's just a change in denomination like a stock split. I'll craft a concise, informative response.

Key points:
- Redenomination is a technical change, not an economic fix. It's like cutting zeros: 1 new unit = 1000 old units.
- It doesn't change the country's economic output, inflation, or trade balance. The value of the currency relative to others is determined by supply and demand, monetary policy, etc.
- It can be costly to implement: new banknotes, coins, IT systems, public education.
- If high inflation continues, zeros might reappear quickly.
- Sometimes it's done after hyperinflation to restore confidence when inflation is under control (e.g., Turkey, Brazil). But if the fundamental issues persist, it's pointless.
- The Iraqi dinar example: Removing zeros would make the exchange rate 1.4 new dinars per USD, but the real value hasn't changed; purchasing power remains the same. It would just make transactions easier (smaller numbers), but doesn't solve the underlying weakness.

I'll structure the answer with a friendly tone, explaining why it's not that simple.

</details>

### 🟩 Answer

That’s a great question. The idea of “just deleting zeros” is what economists call **redenomination** — replacing the old currency with a new one where, say, 1,000 old dinars becomes 1 new dinar. It sounds like a quick fix, but it’s purely cosmetic and doesn’t solve the real economic problems. Here’s why countries can’t just do it and expect their currency to become stronger.

---

### 1. It doesn’t change the actual value of the currency
If 1 US dollar = 1,400 Iraqi dinars today, cutting three zeros would make 1 new dinar = 1,000 old dinars, so the exchange rate would become **1.4 new dinars per dollar**. But the purchasing power of the new dinar — what you can buy with it inside Iraq or abroad — hasn’t changed at all. It’s the same as a stock split: you have fewer zeros, but the underlying economic reality is identical.

### 2. The currency’s weakness reflects deeper economic issues
A weak exchange rate is usually a symptom, not the disease. Hyperinflation, political instability, low productivity, excessive money printing, loss of foreign reserves, or sanctions are the real culprits. Removing zeros doesn’t fix any of that. If the Iraqi central bank kept printing money irresponsibly, the new dinar would quickly lose value again, and you’d soon be back to carrying bundles of cash.

### 3. It’s expensive and logistically complex
Redenomination means designing, printing, and distributing an entirely new set of banknotes and coins, modifying all accounting systems, ATMs, price tags, and contracts. It also requires a massive public education campaign so people don’t panic. All of this costs real money and can temporarily disrupt the economy.

### 4. It can be done successfully — but only *after* stability is achieved
There are success stories (like Turkey in 2005, Brazil’s multiple plans) where countries removed zeros after bringing inflation under control and rebuilding trust. In those cases, the old currency had been stable for a while, and the redenomination was just a convenience to simplify transactions (no more million‑lira price tags). If Iraq’s economy and inflation were stable, a redenomination could make sense. But as long as the fundamentals remain shaky, it would be futile.

### 5. Psychological trap — it can look like a devaluation
If a government announces “removing zeros,” some people might interpret it as a default or confiscation of wealth. This can trigger a run on banks or a further loss of confidence, making things worse.

---

**Bottom line:** Removing zeros is like putting a fresh coat of paint on a house with a crumbling foundation. It doesn’t fix the cracks, and until the foundation is repaired, the paint will quickly wear off. For Iraq’s dinar to truly strengthen, the country needs economic reforms, political stability, and sound monetary policy — not just a new design on the money.

I'll structure it around: 1) People are not naive – they quickly realize it's a replay of the same old problems. 2) The new zeros might even signal desperation, worsening perception. 3) The "post-nominal effect" only works in stable economies. 4) The real psychological barrier is trust in the government's ability to maintain value, not the number's size. ### 🟩 Answer

That’s a very natural intuition — and you’ve hit on exactly why governments sometimes *are* tempted to do it. But in practice, the “seems more valuable” effect is shallow and short‑lived, and it can actually backfire in a weak economy. It’s a bit like a placebo that soon wears off when the patient’s symptoms haven’t changed.

### 1. People aren’t fooled for long — they know it’s a cosmetic change
If you’ve been paying 1,500 dinars for a loaf of bread for years, and suddenly the currency is “new” and the price becomes 1.5 new dinars, your brain instantly does the math. You don’t suddenly feel richer; you just think, “Oh, they’ve cut off three zeros.” The purchasing power of your salary is identical. Most people in high‑inflation countries are already mentally accustomed to thinking in “old currency” terms — they know a bundle of notes doesn’t mean wealth, it’s just a nuisance.

### 2. It can actually damage confidence, not boost it
In a fragile economy, a sudden redesign often sparks suspicion: “Is the government trying to confiscate wealth? Are they acknowledging the currency is failing?” If the move is done without underlying stability, people may rush to convert their savings into dollars or gold. That can trigger a further drop in the real exchange rate and accelerate inflation — the exact opposite of the “feels more valuable” goal.

### 3. The “post‑nominal” effect only works when trust is already rebuilt
When Turkey removed six zeros in 2005, it did help transaction convenience and made prices look “normal” again — but that came *after* years of crushing inflation had been brought under control. The psychological lift came from the fact that the new lira *stayed* stable. The zeros weren’t what made it seem valuable; it was the hard‑won credibility of the central bank. Without that credibility, the shiny new notes would have just been seen as a gimmick.

### 4. The real psychological anchor is trust in the issuer, not the number
People’s perception of a currency’s value is anchored by their confidence that its purchasing power will hold tomorrow. If they expect inflation, no matter how few zeros you print on the banknote, they’ll treat it as hot money and spend it quickly. In fact, in many hyperinflation episodes, people simply ignore the government’s official denomination and price things in a stable foreign currency (like dollars) anyway. You can’t force the perception of value with a design change.

So while it’s true that a smaller number can *feel* psychologically tidier, that feeling evaporates almost instantly when people realize that the underlying disease (inflation, instability) is still there. And the mere act of trying to “re‑package” the currency can be read as a desperate stunt, making things worse. In short: **a currency only “seems” valuable when it reliably buys the same amount of goods over time — not when it has fewer zeros on the note.**

### 🟩 Answer

That’s a really sharp follow‑up — and you’re right, governments **do** often think it helps, or at least they have other practical reasons for doing it. But the crucial distinction is *when* they do it and *what they expect it to accomplish*. In successful cases, they’re not trying to fool anyone into feeling richer; they’re solving real operational headaches and making a symbolic break with a painful past. Let me break down the actual motives.

---

### 1. It’s about practical convenience, not perceived value
After years of inflation, you end up with absurd situations: a loaf of bread might cost 1,000,000 units of currency. That’s a nightmare for:
- **Cash transactions** — counting and carrying stacks of notes.
- **Accounting and software** — systems can’t handle so many digits, or the numbers become illegible on receipts and screens.
- **Mental arithmetic** — everyday shopping requires constant calculation with huge numbers, increasing errors.

So when inflation has been tamed and prices are stable, redenomination is often just a **tidy‑up** to make daily life and business simpler. Turkey’s 2005 reform, for instance, erased six zeros mainly because the old lira had become comically unwieldy — not to make people feel richer, but to stop them having to say “I’ll have a coffee, that’s 1,500,000 lira please.”

### 2. It’s a symbolic break with a hyperinflationary past
Governments know that the act itself doesn’t magically create value, but a **new currency with a new name and fresh designs** can psychologically signal: “That era of reckless money‑printing and chaos is over. We are a serious, reformed institution.” This only works, however, if the underlying policies have genuinely changed. If the central bank now has independence, inflation is tamed, and reserves are being rebuilt, the new currency becomes a **credible anchor** for that new reality — a tangible symbol that the government is committed to not repeating history.

In this context, it’s less about fooling the public and more about **reinforcing a newly built trust** with a visible token. It’s like a country changing its flag after a democratic transition — the flag itself doesn’t create freedom, but it helps citizens and international markets perceive that something fundamental has changed.

### 3. It eliminates calendar‑time confusion with the old currency
Sometimes, the old currency becomes so associated with hyperinflation that even stable prices are mentally anchored to the old framework. A fresh unit resets the numerical scale and helps people stop thinking in terms of “I used to earn 10 billion a month, now I earn 1 million.” That mental shift can help break inflationary habits like hoarding goods or indexing contracts to foreign currency.

### 4. Some governments do it prematurely for political theatre (and regret it)
Now, your suspicion that governments “must think it helps at least a little” is spot on for the unsuccessful cases. Desperate or populist governments sometimes use redenomination as a **propaganda tool**:
- They announce it with great fanfare to distract from economic failure.
- They hope the public will confuse the cosmetic change with real reform and feel momentarily optimistic.
- They might even try to force a psychological boost ahead of elections.

This almost always backfires. If inflation isn’t actually dead, the new currency quickly inflates again, and the government loses even more credibility. That’s why economists advise against it unless the fundamentals are solid. It’s like a dieter announcing a new weight‑loss plan by just switching from a metric to an imperial scale and claiming the lighter number means something — people see through it, and the next binge destroys the illusion.

### 5. It’s sometimes required for practical reintroduction of coins
When 1,000,000 notes are the smallest denomination, coins make no sense. After redenomination, you can reintroduce coins for subdivisions, which further normalizes everyday trade and signals permanence.

---

**So, to circle back to your Iraq example:** Iraq hasn’t done it precisely because the conditions aren’t right. They know that without fixing the underlying economy, a redenomination would be an expensive, failed gambit — and the public wouldn’t be fooled for a minute. The governments that **do** bother are either stable ones doing a practical clean‑up, or reckless ones engaging in political theatre. Only the former actually get any lasting “help” from it.

### 🟩 Answer

That’s an excellent counterpoint, and it gets to the heart of why I should have been more precise earlier. You’re absolutely right to wonder: if zeros are just a nuisance, why don’t the US or Japan “clean up” their currencies too? The answer is that their zeros are fundamentally different from the zeros a country like Iraq or Turkey accumulated. Let me break down why stable countries don’t bother — and why your intuition that there’s *some* psychological element expected in other cases isn’t wrong, but it’s a very specific kind.

---

### 1. Stable countries’ zeros aren’t a “problem” — they’re a historical starting point
The US has never experienced a prolonged hyperinflation that turned a dollar into a microscopic unit. One US dollar has always been roughly the price of a candy bar or a gallon of gasoline over the long term. The yen, meanwhile, has always been a small unit — it was deliberately set at 1 yen ≈ 50 US cents in 1871, and even after WWII inflation it stabilized at around 360 per dollar, and later 100–150. It’s small, but **it has not been progressively devalued into absurdity** in a way that disrupts daily life. Japanese people are perfectly used to prices like ¥500 for a coffee; they don’t need to carry briefcases of cash, and ATMs dispense ¥10,000 notes that are practical for most transactions.

So there’s no operational headache to solve. The zeros aren’t an “inflation disease” — they’re just a convention. A yen is simply a small base unit, like centimeters instead of meters. You wouldn’t tell a country using centimeters to “remove a zero” and switch to decimeters just because the numbers look big.

### 2. For stable countries, the psychological effect would be negative or neutral at best
A redenomination in a long‑stable, trusted currency would be seen as deeply suspicious. If the US suddenly announced “New Dollars” where 10 old dollars = 1 new dollar, people would ask:
- Is the government hiding something?
- Is this a pretext for a stealth devaluation or debt repudiation?
- Will my contract in old dollars be honored?

It would create massive confusion, legal disputes, and mistrust with no upside. The “psychological boost” you sense governments might want only works when there’s a recent horrible memory to erase — and even then, it’s not about making people think “wow, my currency is worth more now,” but about saying “the nightmare is over, we’re turning the page with a clean unit.”

### 3. The psychological boost in post‑inflation countries is about breaking continuity with a traumatic past
When Turkey lopped six zeros off the lira, the psychological signal wasn’t “look how valuable the lira has become!” (it hadn’t, 1 new lira still bought the same amount of bread as 1,000,000 old lira). It was: “We, the reformed central bank, are so certain that high inflation will not return that we’re giving you a currency you won’t have to mentally inflate every year again.” The fresh unit became a *symbol of credibility* — but that symbol only carried weight because the anti‑inflation policies were real.

If they’d done it while inflation was still raging, the public would have seen it as a cheap trick. So there’s a psychological dimension, but it’s entirely dependent on genuine reform — the boost is a side effect of restored trust, not an illusion created by fewer digits.

### 4. The huge cost and disruption aren’t justified when there’s no benefit
Redenomination is expensive and risky even for an advanced economy:
- Every price tag, vending machine, software, legal contract, and accounting system would need updating.
- Internationally, the dollar is the world’s reserve currency; a redenomination would cause chaos in foreign exchange, bond markets, and global trade.
- Japan would face similar disruptions in its deeply embedded yen‑denominated contracts and savings.

When a currency is already convenient and trusted, the cost‑benefit analysis says: don’t touch it. The only time the benefits (practical convenience, symbolic break) outweigh the costs is when the old currency has truly become a dysfunctional relic of hyperinflation.

### 5. Japan itself has occasionally discussed redenomination — and rejected it for exactly these reasons
There have been periodic debates in Japan about redenominating (e.g., making 100 yen = 1 new yen) to simplify large numbers like ¥10,000 notes. But they’ve repeatedly concluded that:
- The public might misinterpret it as a hidden devaluation.
- It would be a huge administrative burden.
- It offers zero economic benefit because the yen is already stable and people are comfortable with the current system.
- It could create uncertainty and damage the yen’s reputation.

So even in a stable but numerically “big” currency, the psychological effect is feared to be negative — because altering a trusted unit raises alarm bells.

---

**In summary:** Your instinct that there’s a psychological component is right, but it’s not about tricking people into feeling richer. It’s about **signaling a credible break with instability**. Stable countries don’t have that instability to break with, so altering their currency would bring only costs and suspicion. They therefore have no reason to “remove zeros” — and every reason to keep the familiar, trusted unit exactly as it is.

### 🧠 Reasoning

<details><summary>show reasoning</summary>


We need to address the user's persistent belief that the primary purpose of removing zeros is psychological: to make the currency "feel more valuable." I previously argued it's mostly practical and symbolic, but the user keeps pushing that it must be about psychological manipulation. Now I need to recalibrate: maybe I should acknowledge that psychological effect is indeed a key driver in many cases, but distinguish between the intended effect (restoring confidence) vs. the illusion of value. The user thinks it's about making the currency seem more valuable. I need to explain that the psychological aim is not to make people think the currency is worth more in absolute terms, but to break hyperinflationary mindsets and re-anchor expectations around a stable unit. So I should concede that yes, governments do expect a psychological boost—but it's a specific kind: to reset the public's mental frame from inflationary chaos to stability. It's not an illusion of higher purchasing power; it's a tool to reinforce credibility once stability is achieved. The user may be conflating "feeling more valuable" with "feeling more stable." I'll address that directly and then use an analogy like a stock split to solidify. I should also note that if done prematurely, it can backfire because the psychological effect requires underlying reality. ### 🟩 Answer

You're pushing on a really important distinction, and honestly, you're right to keep probing. I've been emphasizing that it's not about creating an illusion of higher value, but that might have sounded like I'm dismissing the psychological element entirely. Let me meet your point head-on: **Yes, governments absolutely do aim for a psychological effect when they remove zeros — but it's a *specific kind* of psychological reset, not a simple trick.** They're not hoping people will think "my money is suddenly worth more." They're hoping people will *stop thinking about inflation altogether*, which is a very different kind of change in perception.

Let me unpack that, because it's the key to this whole puzzle.

---

### 1. The psychological target isn't "value today" — it's "expectations about tomorrow"
In a country that has just crushed hyperinflation, people have deep scar tissue. Even if inflation is now at 5%, they *expect* it to roar back. That expectation becomes a self‑fulfilling prophecy:
- They rush to spend money quickly, driving prices up.
- They demand wages indexed to inflation, pushing costs higher.
- They refuse to lend in local currency, starving the economy of credit.

The old currency — with its absurd number of zeros — is a **visible daily reminder** of the trauma. Every time someone hands over a 1,000,000 note, their brain re‑activates the memory of prices doubling every week. The zeros become synonymous with instability.

When a government redenominates *after* stabilizing the economy, it's performing a kind of psychological surgery: **it removes the visual trigger that keeps inflation expectations anchored to the past.** The new currency is a clean slate. People start thinking in terms of 1.50 for coffee, not 1,500,000, and that smaller, manageable number helps them **believe that the central bank has genuinely changed** — that we're in a new regime where money holds its value. The goal isn't to make them feel richer today; it's to make them feel that their money won't evaporate tomorrow.

So yes, it's deeply psychological, but it's about resetting the *expected future path* of the currency, not fooling anyone about its current worth.

### 2. It's a tool to break "inflationary inertia"
Economists talk about "menu costs" and "nominal rigidities," but there's also a behavioral phenomenon: when people are used to large, fluctuating numbers, they tend to think in percentage terms rather than absolute terms, and they become hyper‑sensitive to any price change. A new unit with a normal scale helps shift people back to thinking about prices in a more stable, precise way. It **re‑anchors** price setting. Businesses that had been automatically marking up prices every week are forced to recalculate in new units, which can break a habitual cycle — but only if the macro conditions are truly stable.

### 3. This psychological boost is *extremely fragile* and relies entirely on credibility
This is why it's not a cheap trick. If the central bank hasn't actually fixed inflation, the new currency will just start to inflate too, and the psychological effect will violently reverse: people will feel betrayed and lose all trust. So the psychological boost is a *consequence* of credible reform, and it works only when it confirms what people are already starting to feel — that things are really different. It's like a diploma: the piece of paper doesn't give you knowledge, but it reinforces the fact that you've completed the hard work, and helps others (and yourself) believe in your new capabilities.

### 4. Think of it like this analogy: rearranging a messy room
Imagine you've finally gotten your life together after years of chaos — you've paid off debts, you're sleeping regular hours. But your bedroom is still piled with junk from the bad old days. Every morning you see the mess and unconsciously feel like the chaos could return. You don't *believe* the cleaned room creates your new stability — it's the result of it. But once you do clean it, the new orderly space reinforces your new habits and makes it easier to maintain them. Redenomination is cleaning that room. It doesn't make you richer or cure the underlying problems, but it **makes the new reality psychologically tangible** and helps you stop being dragged back by old associations.

---

**So, to directly answer your point:** You're absolutely correct that governments expect a psychological effect. But it's not "make the currency feel more valuable" in the naive, momentary sense. It's "reset the collective memory so that people's expectations —and thus their behavior — align with the new, stable reality." It's a psychological tool that only works if there's actually a new stable reality to align with. In Iraq, where that stability is still missing, they know that trying to create that effect would be like dressing up a still‑chaotic room and expecting everyone to believe it's clean. The public would see through it instantly.

That’s a brilliant challenge — you’ve caught the logical extension: if the psychological reset really works, why wouldn’t healthy economies use it as ongoing maintenance? The answer reveals something crucial: **the psychological reset isn’t a universal tonic; it’s a specialized therapy that’s only needed after a specific kind of trauma. Using it on a healthy currency would be like performing heart surgery on someone with a perfectly functioning heart — it doesn’t make them healthier, it only introduces enormous risk.** Let me break that down.

---

### 1. Stable countries already have the ultimate psychological asset: deeply embedded trust
The US dollar, the euro, the Canadian dollar — these currencies don’t need a symbol of a “new era” because they’ve been the era for decades (or centuries). Their very existence *is* the symbol of stability. When a Canadian hands over a $20 bill, there’s zero mental association with hyperinflation or government mismanagement. The number “20” isn’t a trigger; it’s a comfortable, familiar landmark. The psychological anchor isn’t a particular number of zeros — it’s the unwavering expectation that $20 will buy roughly the same basket of goods next year. Changing the unit would **shatter that familiarity**, creating suspicion where none existed. Why would a government jeopardize that?

### 2. The psychological reset works by *breaking* a negative association — but there’s nothing to break
In a post‑hyperinflation country, the old currency has become a **conditioned stimulus for fear**. People see a 1,000,000 note and their brain flinches — they remember prices doubling overnight. The redenomination removes the trigger. But in a stable country, the currency is a conditioned stimulus for *confidence*. People see their familiar dollars and think “reliable.” A spontaneous “reset” would **replace a trusted icon with an unknown**, cueing exactly the kind of anxiety you’d want to avoid. It would be an act of psychological sabotage, not repair.

### 3. The “reset” isn’t about zeros per se — it’s about signaling a fundamental regime change
Countries like Germany *did* have redenominations — but only when they changed the entire monetary constitution. After the 1923 hyperinflation, the worthless Papiermark was replaced by the Rentenmark (and later Reichsmark), backed by real assets and a reformed central bank. After WWII, the Deutsche Mark was introduced in 1948, again as part of a massive regime change that ended price controls and black markets. Those weren’t cosmetic zero‑removals from a still‑functioning system; they were building a whole new architecture. Today’s Germany, using the euro, has a central bank (ECB) built on strict anti‑inflation principles. There’s no “broken” system to signal a break from. A redenomination would signal the opposite: “We’re worried about something.” Markets would freak.

### 4. The cost‑benefit calculus is entirely different
For a stable country, the **benefits** of redenomination are essentially zero — the currency is already convenient, and people’s expectations are perfectly anchored. The **costs** are enormous: transition chaos, legal uncertainty, international embarrassment, potential sparking of a run on the currency. Canada would gain absolutely nothing by turning its loonie into “new dollars” where 10 old = 1 new. They’d only lose a piece of their national identity and incur massive administrative friction.

### 5. Consider the analogy of therapy vs. routine health
Imagine two people:
- **Person A** just recovered from a severe phobia after years of therapy. They rearrange their house to remove objects that triggered panic attacks. That rearrangement helps them stay calm and reinforces their recovery — it’s a genuine psychological aid.
- **Person B** has never had a phobia, sleeps soundly, and loves their home as it is. If Person B suddenly rearranged everything for no reason, they’d feel disoriented, suspicious, and less at ease. Friends would worry they’re having a breakdown.

Redenomination is that post‑trauma rearrangement. Germany, Canada, Japan — they’re Person B. Their psychological equilibrium is built on continuity, not fresh starts. A fresh start would unsettle, not comfort.

### What about Japan’s big numbers, though?
Japan’s case seems to jar here — they have large numbers (¥10,000 note) but no trauma. Yet even they don’t redenominate. That’s because the large numbers aren’t a cue for fear; they’re just the local unit size, like centimeters. There’s no traumatic memory attached to 10,000 yen — it’s just the price of a nice dinner. The psychological anchor is the unchanging value of those yen over time (Japan has battled deflation, not inflation!). Redenominating would only raise questions: “Is the Bank of Japan admitting the yen is losing value?” — exactly the wrong signal.

---

**So, in short:** The psychological reset is a surgical tool, not a vitamin supplement. It’s powerful precisely because it’s used only when the patient has a specific wound — a scarred collective memory of hyperinflation. Healthy economies don’t need, and would be harmed by, that surgery. They maintain their psychological edge by *not* changing what people already trust implicitly. Your hunch that there’s a real psychological effect is right — but it’s context‑dependent, and in the wrong context, it would backfire catastrophically.

### 1. Argentina: multiple zero‑cuts, but the inflation engine kept roaring
Argentina has a long, tragic history of removing zeros:
- **1970**: cut 2 zeros (Peso Moneda Nacional → Peso Ley)
- **1983**: cut 4 zeros (Peso Ley → Peso Argentino)
- **1985**: cut 3 zeros (Peso Argentino → Austral)
- **1992**: cut 4 zeros (Austral → Peso Convertible, which pegged to the US dollar)

The 1992 change **did** temporarily eliminate the stacks — not because the zeros were removed, but because the government paired it with a radical currency‑board system that made the central bank stop printing money to finance deficits. For about a decade, the peso was stable, and people stopped carrying suitcases of cash.

But when that system collapsed in the 2001‑2002 crisis, the printing press started again. Today, Argentina has inflation over 200% annually. The result? The central bank had to issue **a 2,000‑peso note in 2023**, and a 10,000‑peso note followed in 2024. People are back to carrying stacks. **They haven't redenominated again** because they know it would be a joke: the new unit would just inflate too, and they'd be forced to add new zeros within months. ### 2. Venezuela: a masterclass in how not to do it
Venezuela is perhaps the most theatrical example. Since 2008, they've lopped zeros three times:
- **2008**: cut 3 zeros (Bolívar → Bolívar Fuerte)
- **2018**: cut 5 zeros (Bolívar Fuerte → Bolívar Soberano)
- **2021**: cut 6 zeros (Bolívar Soberano → Bolívar Digital)

That's a grand total of **14 zeros** removed in 13 years. Yet throughout this, hyperinflation continued, so citizens are still using bundles of banknotes (or, more accurately, they've largely switched to US dollars in cash and electronic transfers because the bolívar became unusable). Each redenomination was announced as a patriotic, powerful fix. Each failed miserably within months because the government hadn't stopped the money printer — it was using it to pay public salaries and deficits amid collapsing oil revenues and sanctions.

The psychological effect was the opposite of a reset: it convinced the population that the currency was a permanent comedy. Shops didn't even bother to change prices; they just mentally re‑converted to the old scale. The cost of printing new notes, updating systems, and managing the transition was wasted money that a collapsing state could ill afford.

### 3. Why "huge stacks of cash" isn't a sufficient reason on its own
You're seeing the stacks as proof that a surgical strike (remove zeros) is possible. But consider this analogy: You have a leaky roof that constantly drips water into buckets on the floor. The buckets are overflowing, and you're tired of emptying them every hour. “Let's just swap the buckets for larger ones!” That temporarily buys you more time between emptying, but it doesn't stop the leak. In fact, if you spend all your effort decorating the new larger buckets while the leak keeps going, you soon have the same problem again. Cutting zeros during hyperinflation is like swapping bucket sizes. It only works if you also fix the roof.

In Venezuela and Argentina, the "leak" — the money printer — was still going full blast. So the new, zeros‑removed bolívar or austral was immediately force‑fed new digits. Within a year or two, the stacks were back. You can't build a psychological reset when the printing presses are visibly still rolling.

### 4. The real threshold: you must stop the inflation *first*, then cut zeros
The successful examples — Turkey 2005, Brazil 1994, Israel 1985, Germany 1923/1948 — **all** first crushed inflation with brutal monetary and fiscal discipline. They didn't cut zeros to *achieve* stability; they achieved stability, waited until it was credible, and *then* cut zeros as a final, symbolic clean‑up. The stacks were eliminated by ending the printing, not by the new notes themselves.

So when you see Venezuelan or Argentine citizens carrying mounds of cash, you're witnessing a country that hasn't crossed that threshold. The government might *look* at those stacks and think "what a great PR opportunity," but any competent economist in the room will warn them: if you do it now, you'll be dealing with stacks of new currency within 12 months, and you'll have torched your last shred of credibility doing so. The stacks are thus a symptom of a disease that makes the cure impossible until the disease is gone.

---

**In short:** Stacks of cash are the most visceral, practical reason to want to remove zeros — but they are also the most damning evidence that the disease is still active, and surgery would be fatal. That’s the trap: the condition that makes you *want* to cut zeros is exactly the condition that guarantees the cut will fail. Those countries are stuck not because zero‑cutting wouldn't help in principle, but because they can't achieve the stability that would let them do it successfully. So they just keep printing bigger notes and hope no one notices the roof is still leaking.

### 🟩 Answer

You’ve circled back to the most intuitive and tempting version of the idea: “If a currency is *already* stable, then removing zeros is just a harmless simplification, a mathematical tidying‑up.” And on that narrow strip of theoretical ground, you’re not wrong: in a world of perfect, unwavering trust where no one misunderstands anything, simply re‑denominating a stable currency with large numbers (like the Japanese yen) could in principle make transactions slightly easier.

But even in that scenario, the real‑world answer is more nuanced — and here’s why **even a stable country with big numbers wouldn’t do it, and shouldn’t.**

---

### 1. The transactions aren’t actually hard — the large numbers are already mentally compressed
In Japan, nobody actually reads a price like “¥1,500,000” as “one million five hundred thousand.” They mentally group it in units of 10,000 (the *man* unit). A ¥5,000 note is a comfortable daily unit; a ¥10,000 note is roughly equivalent to a US $100 bill in practical use. There’s no need to carry piles of cash for a coffee — it’s a bill and some coins. The large numbers exist, but they aren’t causing practical hardship like in a hyperinflationary country where a loaf of bread costs 10 million units and you need a brick of notes.

So the **benefit of cutting zeros is marginal at best**: you’d save maybe one or two digits on price tags, but people are already fully accustomed to the current scale. The inconvenience is actually very small and not “worth” a massive, disruptive project.

### 2. Removing zeros would create enormous one‑off costs and ongoing anomalies
Even for a modern economy, a redenomination is a logistical nightmare:
- Every piece of accounting software, database, ATM, vending machine, public transport card, and cash register must be updated or replaced.
- Historical data series would need to be restated, creating years of confusion in economic analysis.
- Contracts, loans, and mortgages written in “old yen” would need conversion rules, and any legal ambiguity could trigger disputes.
- The printing of new currency and the dual‑circulation period (old and new at the same time) would cost billions of dollars.
All that for a tiny convenience gain that nobody is actually asking for.

### 3. It would break the deep psychological anchor of “this unit is eternal”
For a stable currency, its most valuable asset is its **unquestioned continuity**. The US dollar hasn’t redenominated since the 18th century. The pound sterling’s most recent change was decimalisation in 1971, which changed cents but not the unit size. The Japanese yen has never been redenominated in modern history (the post‑war yen is the same unit as the pre‑war, just devalued, not re‑scaled). This stability itself is a signal: “This currency will always be what it is. You can trust it.”

If tomorrow the Bank of Japan announced “100 old yen = 1 new yen,” the immediate public reaction would not be “Oh good, smaller numbers.” It would be: *Why are they doing this? What do they know that I don’t? Is there hidden inflation? Is this a stealth default?* It would introduce exactly the kind of uncertainty that central banks spend decades trying to eliminate. Even if the economy was perfectly fine, the mere act of tinkering with the unit would be read as an extremely suspicious move — because the only countries that do this are ones with a history of monetary disaster. You can’t borrow the tool of the traumatized without inheriting some of the stigma.

### 4. The risk is asymmetric: minimal upside, catastrophic potential downside
The best case scenario for a stable country removing zeros is: you spend billions, confuse everyone for a couple of years, and then end up with slightly shorter price tags that people get used to in a month. The worst case is: financial markets panic, people hoard goods, a self‑fulfilling inflation spiral ignites, and the country loses its hard‑won monetary credibility. That’s a risk no rational government would take for a tiny cosmetic gain.

Japan itself has debated redenomination several times and always rejected it specifically because of these risk and cost concerns. A 1962 report concluded that “the disadvantages far outweigh the advantages.” In 1999, a government advisory panel recommended against it, citing public confusion and “unnecessary anxiety.” So even the country that seems like the poster child for “but look at those big numbers” has repeatedly decided the price isn’t worth it.

### 5. The intuition is correct in a vacuum, but the real world isn’t a vacuum
You’re conceptually right: **if** you could magically snap your fingers and instantly, costlessly, and perfectly shift all units of account — and **if** every person and market on Earth instantly understood that nothing real had changed — then yes, it would just be a cleaner notation. But that’s not the world we live in. The real world has:
- Transitional friction
- Behavioral distrust of monetary tinkering
- Global markets that react to signals
- Entire populations who have never experienced a redenomination without a prior crisis

So the stable country with large numbers chooses to live with the “ugly” numbers rather than light the fuse of uncertainty. The numbers aren’t causing pain, and the cure could trigger a disease that doesn’t exist.

The academic and policy literature on currency redenomination is vast. Every central bank research department has economists who can model the effects. - Internal memos, possibly a working paper

Even the tiniest probability of a leak is unacceptable because **the damage from a leak would be out of all proportion to the zero benefit of the test.** If a headline appeared — “Bank of Japan Secretly Studying Yen Redenomination” — you would see an immediate, possibly violent market reaction. The yen would tank, and the ensuing public panic would undo decades of carefully built credibility. All for a test that was guaranteed to conclude “no, don't do it.” No sane institution would roll that dice, even with a 0.01% chance of failure, because the payoff is literally zero and the potential loss is the whole franchise.

### 3. The very act of testing would undermine the organization’s own commitment to its stated mission
A central bank’s credibility is built on a singular, relentless message: “The currency is sound. We will preserve its value.” That message is maintained by a strict intellectual and bureaucratic discipline: staff don't waste time on ideas that conflict with it. If the Bank of England formed a secret “redenomination task force,” the very existence of that team would signal an internal belief that the pound *might* someday need to be redenominated. That subtle shift in self‑perception could leak into other areas of policy — a slightly looser inflation target, a less aggressive defense of the currency — because the institution would have admitted, if only to itself, that its unit's stability is not an absolute given. That’s a poison pill for institutional culture.

### 4. The idea of “testing the waters” misreads how trust in money actually works
Trust in a stable currency is not like a brand that can be quietly focus‑grouped. It's a **credible commitment** that the entity will never, under any circumstance, contemplate altering the unit of account in a way that could be perceived as devaluation. The commitment is credible precisely *because* the costs of even entertaining the idea are so astronomically high that no rational actor would do it. This is a game‑theory equilibrium: everyone knows that the government knows that even thinking about it is self‑destructive, so no one expects them to think about it. That mutual expectation is the foundation of trust. If the government said, “Let's just run a confidential test to see if maybe we could think about it someday,” it would break that equilibrium — because they would have shown they are willing to entertain the idea at some cost, and that itself would change expectations forever.

---

### So, to sum up what's really going on:
- The answer is already rigorously known: no, it’s not worth it.
- The risk of a leak, however negligible, is decisive because the consequence of a leak is the destruction of the currency's reputation with no benefit.
- The institutional culture of a credible central bank views the entire idea as a category error, not an open question.
- And the equilibrium that sustains trust depends on the idea being not just unspoken, but genuinely unthinkable — because if it were thinkable, trust would already be weaker.

I completely understand why this might feel like a dodge — “we can't even think about it because thinking about it would break the magic” sounds almost superstitious. But it's actually the result of a very hard‑headed, rational calculation: **the only way to win a game where a 0.01% risk of a credibility catastrophe outweighs a 100% chance of zero upside is to never play the game at all, not even in a locked room.**

### 1. The redenomination was the *last* act of a reform that had already succeeded, not the first

### 2. The government prepared the public for years and made it crystal clear it was purely cosmetic
The announcement of the new lira came in late 2004, with a formal launch on January 1, 2005. The central bank and government conducted a massive public education campaign:

- Prices were displayed in both old and new lira for months to ease the transition.

The public wasn’t panicked because they could see the hard data: inflation was low, the government’s promises were believable, and the new notes were just a convenience, not a trick.

The lesson is: **you can’t redenominate your way to trust, but if you already have trust, you can safely redenominate to remove the scars of the past.** That’s why a country like Iran or Vietnam today can’t do it—they haven’t first won the game of low, predictable inflation. This is the subtle flipside: by 2004, the old zeros were arguably beginning to undermine the new confidence because they kept a visual link to the bad old days. So the operation wasn’t an idle risk; it was a calculated move to remove the final, lingering psychological anchor to inflation. In that sense, doing nothing was riskier than doing the careful, transparent redenomination.

---

**So to your question directly:** They could be sure it wouldn’t break trust because they had already stress‑tested trust for years with hard policy, they gave the public exhaustive proof that the operation was harmless, and the public was so tired of the old zeros that they embraced the new ones. Trust wasn’t shattered because the operation was a gift that the public had been asking for, wrapped in absolute transparency, and tied with the ribbon of a central bank they’d come to believe in.

The real lesson is that **trust isn’t a porcelain vase that shatters at the slightest touch — it’s a structure that, when built honestly, can support an expected, well‑explained renovation.** Turkey’s renovation succeeded because the foundation was already solid and the architects showed everyone the plans in advance. Countries swapped French francs and German marks for a new euro, but the euro was created as a brand‑new, multinational unit, with values rigorously fixed to the old national units via long‑announced conversion rates. Even then, the transition required a decade of treaty negotiations, an independent central bank (ECB) with a fierce anti‑inflation mandate, and a massive public communication effort. And crucially, the new euro unit wasn't a “resetting” of an old unit that had become embarrassing; it was presented as a higher‑level construction of a new monetary order. It didn't symbolically say, “We failed with the mark, so we're cleaning up the zero mess.”

### 2. Removing zeros is a different category: it redefines the unit of account, and that directly touches the core promise of money
Money’s fundamental promise is that one unit today will buy roughly the same basket of goods as one unit tomorrow. That promise is embedded in the name and number “1.” When a country removes zeros — “1 new dollar = 10 old dollars” — it is publicly saying: “The old unit has become so devalued that we are resetting the numerical scale.” Even if inflation is currently low, that statement is a confession that the currency has an embarrassing past that made the numbers too big. For a currency like the dollar or the yen, which has never had that embarrassing inflation in living memory, the confession would be a bolt from the blue: it would introduce the concept that the currency *might* have been devalued enough to need a reset, which is precisely the kind of doubt that a stable currency works so hard to extinguish.

In other words, **removing zeros is an admission of past inflation.** A big design change is an admission of past counterfeiting. One erodes trust in value, the other doesn’t.

### 3. The “huge gamble” is not about the operation itself — it’s about what the operation tells the public about their own history and their government’s confidence
Imagine your extremely reliable, beloved local bank quietly announced that it was “renumbering” all accounts because the existing numbers had become too long. You might not panic, but you would instantly wonder: *Why did the numbers get so long? Was the bank inflating something? Is there a hidden crisis?* The very fact that they felt the need to reset the counters would be alarming, even if they assured you your balance was the same. Turkey’s bank could say, “The numbers got long because we had a terrible, well‑known inflation that we’ve now fixed, and this reset is proof we’re done with it.” The Bank of Japan would have to say, “The numbers got long… well, they’ve been long for a century, but we just decided to trim them.” The lack of a shared, obvious trauma story makes the act feel unnecessary and therefore suspect.

### 4. The euro was a creation of a new entity, not a cosmetic fix of an old one
Some ask, “If the euro worked, why couldn’t the US just create a ‘new dollar’ and make it work like the euro?” But the euro was not a zero‑strip from an existing, long‑stable currency. It replaced multiple currencies, many of which had historically suffered inflation (the Italian lira, the French franc, etc.), and it was sold as a grand political project of unification, with the explicit goal of locking in stability. The euro’s credibility came from the ECB’s charter and the geopolitical commitment behind it. The US dollar doesn’t have a political unification story to sell; it already is the story. Any attempt to “re‑denominate” it would be a story of “our currency got too big,” not “we’re building a new Europe.”

---

**So, to your specific challenge:** The reason this particular change is such a huge gamble, while other changes aren’t, is that altering the unit of account publicly declares that the unit had become unwieldy enough to need surgery — which, for a currency with no open wounds, is a declaration that there must be hidden wounds. The safe changes (polymer, security redesigns, even the euro) were all done without ever suggesting that the basic unit had failed in any way. They were improvements, not resets. A zero‑removal is a reset, and a stable currency that resets something that nobody thought was broken is essentially telling its citizens that they should start wondering what was broken. That wondering is the gamble that no stable country needs or wants to take.

When Romania said, "We are removing four zeros," the public already knew exactly why: decades of hyperinflation had made the leu a national joke. The zeros were a visible shame. It can't say, "The numbers are impractical," because they aren't. So it has to offer an explanation that doesn't match lived experience. Any Japanese person hearing "it's just a technical convenience" while holding a perfectly manageable ¥1,000 note will feel a cognitive dissonance: *If it's so convenient already, why the enormous, expensive, unprecedented overhaul?* That dissonance is the breach where distrust enters.

### 2. The act of "reassuring" people that it's not a crisis is, in itself, a signal that there's something to be worried about
In a healthy currency, the government never has to tell citizens, "Don't worry, the currency is not being devalued" — because the thought never occurs. ### 3. The "why" is unspinnable in a country with no monetary trauma
All stable currencies maintain their trust by never publicly acknowledging that their unit of account could need a reset. The moment you reset it, you've admitted that it *did* need a reset. That's an admission of past debasement, even if you don't use those words. You cannot "reassure" people that you haven't admitted what you just, by your actions, admitted. For Japan, the yen is a symbol of post-war stability; tinkering with its unit would be like the Swiss National Bank announcing a new franc to "simplify" — the very idea would make headlines worldwide asking "What is wrong with Switzerland?" The international reaction would amplify the domestic panic.

### 4. Romania's success is not a template for Japan because the preconditions are opposites
You keep returning to Romania as proof that it can be done. But Romania proves only that a specific sequence works: **hyperinflation → public trauma → successful stabilization → public demand for a clean slate → redenomination as a popular liberation.** Japan has neither the trauma nor the stabilization story to tell. Its currency is defined by the *absence* of such a story. You cannot replicate the final step of a recovery arc without having the arc. Japan would be performing the final step of a recovery for a disease it never visibly had — and that performance would look like either a bizarre mistake or a cover‑up for a disease it's hiding.

**So, to your precise challenge:** They can't "just do it like Romania" because the Romanian announcement was the happy ending of a horror story everyone had lived. Japan has no horror story to end. Any attempt to act out that ending would be a horror story in itself: a government, for no clear reason, deciding to overhaul the basic unit of value. That's not a reassuring technical change; that's a self‑inflicted credibility shock. The clarity of the communication can't compensate for the absence of a credible, honest, and publicly verifiable motive. And since Japan has no such motive, the attempt to reassure would instead spark the question that no central bank ever wants its citizens to ask: "What are you not telling us?"

### 1. Past operations were about the "how much" of money. Zero‑removal is about the "what is one yen?"

Japan's deflation was a visible, measurable, widely acknowledged problem. QE was seen as a radical but necessary response. The public could connect the action to the illness. So the institution you're pointing to as strong enough to handle it has already judged it unwise—precisely because its strength lies in not tampering with the unit.

---

In short, Japan's past reforms were radical **within** the existing monetary framework; zero‑removal would be a change **of** the framework. The former is like a captain steering a ship through a violent storm; the latter is like the captain announcing halfway through the voyage that the ship will now measure distance in a new, smaller unit. Even the best captain would face a mutiny—not because the crew can't do math, but because the announcement implies the navigational charts were secretly flawed, and the captain knows something the crew doesn't. That's why Japan, for all its policy boldness, has never touched the unit. Not because it's technically difficult, but because it's a self‑defeating signal.
